Fundamentals Q62 of 209
What do honor_labels and honor_timestamps do in a scrape_config?
A) honor_labels makes Prometheus respect the target's labels; honor_timestamps makes Prometheus respect the target's scrape_interval
B) honor_labels (default false): if true, target's labels override Prometheus's labels when they conflict (e.g., target says job="custom" → keeps it instead of scrape_config's job_name). honor_timestamps (default true): if true, Prometheus uses the timestamp from the target's /metrics response; if false, Prometheus uses its own scrape timestamp
C) Both are deprecated and no longer used in Prometheus 2.x
D) honor_labels controls whether to add labels; honor_timestamps controls whether to add timestamps
✅ Correct Answer: B

honor_labels (default: false): When a target's /metrics already includes a label that Prometheus would normally set (like job or instance), should the target's value win? false = Prometheus overrides (safe default). true = target's label wins (useful for federation where the downstream Prometheus already has meaningful job/instance labels). honor_timestamps (default: true): Should Prometheus use the timestamp provided in the /metrics exposition format, or its own scrape timestamp? true = trust the target (default, good for accurate event timing). false = use Prometheus's scrape time (useful when target clocks are unreliable).

📝 Detailed Explanation
honor_labels vs honor_timestamps
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  honor_labels: false (default)                                     │
│    Target returns: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42       │
│    Prometheus overrides: job → scrape_config job_name             │
│                          instance → target host:port              │
│    Final: metric{job="anihpj-api",instance="10.244.1.23:8000"} 42│
│                                                                  │
│  honor_labels: true                                                │
│    Target returns: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42       │
│    Prometheus respects target labels: job="custom" stays          │
│    Final: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42                │
│    ⚠️ Can cause label conflicts — use carefully!                  │
│                                                                  │
│  honor_timestamps: true (default)                                  │
│    Target returns: metric{...} 42 1718387535                      │
│    Prometheus uses: 1718387535 (target's timestamp)               │
│    ✅ Good: accurate event timing                                 │
│                                                                  │
│  honor_timestamps: false                                           │
│    Target returns: metric{...} 42 1718387535                      │
│    Prometheus ignores target timestamp                            │
│    Prometheus uses: own scrape timestamp (e.g., 1718387550)       │
│    ✅ Good: when target clocks are unreliable                     │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 honor_labels=true: useful for federation                      │
│  💡 honor_timestamps=false: useful for unreliable target clocks   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: honor_labels and honor_timestamps are scrape_config fields tested on the PCA. Know their defaults and when to change them (federation for honor_labels, unreliable clocks for honor_timestamps).

Fundamentals Q63 of 209
How do you reload Prometheus configuration without restarting the server, and what flag must be enabled?
A) Send HTTP POST to /-/reload (or SIGHUP signal to the process). Requires Prometheus to be started with --web.enable-lifecycle flag. This hot-reloads prometheus.yml, rule files, and target definitions without dropping in-flight scrapes or queries
B) Run prometheus --reload from the command line — it sends a reload signal to the running process
C) Edit prometheus.yml and Prometheus detects the file change automatically via inotify within 15 seconds
D) Prometheus cannot be reloaded — you must restart the process for any config change
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus supports hot-reloading via POST /-/reload or sending SIGHUP (kill -HUP) to the process. The --web.enable-lifecycle flag MUST be set at startup — without it, /-/reload returns 404. On reload, Prometheus re-reads prometheus.yml, rule files, and target definitions, applying changes without restarting. In-flight scrapes and queries continue uninterrupted. For anihpj on Kubernetes: the Prometheus Operator automatically POSTs to /-/reload when ServiceMonitor CRDs change, making config updates seamless. Manual reload: curl -X POST http://prometheus:9090/-/reload.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Config Reload — Hot Reload Flow
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  METHOD 1: HTTP POST /-/reload                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  $ curl -X POST http://localhost:9090/-/reload                    │
│  → Prometheus re-reads prometheus.yml                             │
│  → Prometheus re-reads rule files                                 │
│  → Prometheus updates scrape targets                              │
│  → Response: 200 OK (or 404 if --web.enable-lifecycle not set)    │
│                                                                  │
│  METHOD 2: SIGHUP signal                                           │
│                                                                  │
│  $ kill -HUP $(pidof prometheus)                                   │
│  → Same effect as POST /-/reload                                  │
│  → Useful in non-containerized environments                      │
│                                                                  │
│  WHAT GETS RELOADED:                                               │
│    ✅ prometheus.yml (scrape configs, global settings)            │
│    ✅ Rule files (recording rules, alerting rules)                │
│    ✅ Target definitions (new targets discovered, old removed)    │
│                                                                  │
│  WHAT DOES NOT GET RELOADED:                                       │
│    ❌ Command-line flags (--storage.tsdb.path, etc.) — restart req│
│    ❌ Remote write/read endpoints — requires restart              │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 --web.enable-lifecycle MUST be set (not default!)             │
│  💡 SIGHUP is the traditional Unix way; /-/reload is HTTP-native │
│  💡 Prometheus Operator does this automatically via CRD changes  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Config reload is a fundamental Prometheus operation. Know: POST /-/reload (or SIGHUP), --web.enable-lifecycle must be enabled, and what gets reloaded vs what requires a restart.

2.3 Understanding LimitationsQ64-Q71

Fundamentals Q64 of 209
What is Prometheus NOT designed for, and what tools should you use instead for those use cases?
A) Prometheus is a complete observability platform — it handles metrics, logs, traces, and dashboards natively
B) Prometheus only lacks authentication — everything else is built-in and production-ready at any scale
C) Prometheus is NOT designed for: long-term storage (use Thanos/Cortex/Mimir), high availability by itself (run 2+ instances), logs (use Loki), traces (use Tempo), dashboarding (use Grafana), horizontal scaling (use federation or Thanos), authentication (use reverse proxy/oauth2-proxy). Prometheus excels at real-time metrics collection + short-term storage + alerting — it's purpose-built, not all-purpose
D) Prometheus is designed to replace Elasticsearch, Grafana, and Jaeger — it's an all-in-one platform
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus is purpose-built, not all-purpose. It excels at: real-time metrics scraping, short-term TSDB storage (days to weeks), PromQL querying, and alerting rule evaluation. It is NOT designed for: long-term storage (local retention is limited by disk — use Thanos/Cortex/Mimir for years), high availability (run 2+ identical instances + Thanos Query dedup), logs (use Loki/Elasticsearch), traces (use Tempo/Jaeger), dashboarding (use Grafana — Prometheus /graph is primitive), horizontal scaling (use federation or sharding + Thanos), and authentication (use reverse proxy like oauth2-proxy). Knowing these limitations is as important as knowing its strengths.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Limitations — What It Doesn't Do
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PROMETHEUS DOES:              PROMETHEUS DOESN'T DO:             │
│  ─────────────────────         ────────────────────────           │
│  ✅ Scrape metrics (Pull)      ❌ Long-term storage (use Thanos)  │
│  ✅ Short-term TSDB (15-30d)   ❌ HA by itself (run 2 instances)  │
│  ✅ PromQL queries             ❌ Dashboarding (use Grafana)      │
│  ✅ Alerting rules             ❌ Log aggregation (use Loki)      │
│  ✅ Service discovery          ❌ Distributed tracing (use Tempo) │
│  ✅ Federation                 ❌ Authentication (use oauth2)     │
│                                ❌ Horizontal scaling (use Thanos) │
│                                ❌ Multi-tenancy (use Cortex/Mimir)│
│                                                                  │
│  THE MODERN STACK:                                                │
│    Prometheus (metrics) + Loki (logs) + Tempo (traces)            │
│    + Grafana (dashboards) + Alertmanager (routing)               │
│    + Thanos/Mimir (long-term, HA, global scale)                  │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus is a component, not a platform                    │
│  💡 PCA exam tests your knowledge of these boundaries            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests Prometheus limitations explicitly. Know what Prometheus does NOT do and which tools fill those gaps — this is a frequently tested topic.

Fundamentals Q65 of 209
Why can't a single Prometheus instance handle millions of targets or unlimited cardinality?
A) Prometheus has a hardcoded limit of 10,000 targets — it refuses to scrape more
B) The problem is purely network bandwidth — Prometheus itself can handle any scale
C) The problem is only disk space — with enough disk, Prometheus scales infinitely
D) Each unique time series (metric + labels) consumes ~3-5KB RAM in the head block. A single instance is practically limited to ~10 million active series on high-end hardware. Beyond that: OOM kills, slow queries, compaction failures. This is why sharding, federation, and Thanos/Cortex exist for larger scale
✅ Correct Answer: D

Prometheus is a single-node TSDB — it doesn't shard or distribute data across nodes. Each unique time series (e.g., http_requests_total{job="api", method="GET", status="200", endpoint="/jobs"} is ONE series) consumes ~3-5KB of RAM in the head block. 10 million active series ≈ 30-50GB RAM — the practical limit on a beefy server. Beyond that: OOM kills, PromQL queries time out (they scan all series), compaction falls behind, WAL grows unboundedly. For scale beyond one instance: use sharding (split targets across multiple Prometheus instances), federation (hierarchical aggregation), or Thanos/Cortex (distributed TSDB). For anihpj (thousands of series, not millions), a single instance is more than sufficient.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Scaling Limits
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SINGLE PROMETHEUS INSTANCE LIMITS:                                │
│                                                                  │
│  Active Series    RAM Usage    Status                             │
│  ─────────────    ─────────    ──────────────────────────────    │
│  < 1 million      < 5GB        ✅ Comfortable (most setups)       │
│  1-5 million      5-25GB       ✅ Manageable (anihpj scale)       │
│  5-10 million     25-50GB      ⚠️ Needs careful tuning            │
│  10-20 million    50-100GB     🔴 Pushing limits                   │
│  > 20 million     > 100GB      💀 OOM kill likely                  │
│                                                                  │
│  SCALING STRATEGIES:                                              │
│    → Sharding: split targets across N Prometheus instances        │
│    → Federation: global aggregates from regional instances        │
│    → Thanos/Cortex: distributed TSDB, global query view           │
│    → Drop labels: metric_relabel_configs to reduce cardinality    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 1 series = 1 unique metric+labels combination                │
│  💡 Each series = 3-5KB RAM in head block                        │
│  💡 Cardinality explosion = #1 cause of OOM kills                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding Prometheus scaling limits is critical. Know that ~10M series is the practical limit per instance, and which strategies (sharding, Thanos) solve scaling beyond that.

Fundamentals Q66 of 209
What is cardinality explosion and why is it the #1 cause of Prometheus OOM kills?
A) Cardinality = number of unique label value combinations. Explosion happens when unbounded labels (user_id, email, IP) create thousands of unique series per metric. 1 metric × 100,000 user_ids = 100,000 series consuming ~500MB RAM. Prometheus OOM-kills because the head block can't hold all series in memory. Solution: drop high-cardinality labels via metric_relabel_configs
B) Cardinality explosion means too many metrics in a single scrape — it's solved by increasing scrape_timeout
C) Cardinality is only a problem if you have more than 100 targets — below that, it's irrelevant
D) Cardinality explosion happens when Prometheus scrapes too frequently — reducing scrape_interval fixes it
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cardinality is the total number of unique time series. Explosion happens when an unbounded label creates astronomical series counts. Example: you add user_id as a label. anihpj has 100,000 users. Every metric now gets multiplied by 100,000: http_requests_total alone becomes 100,000 series (one per user) instead of ~20 (by endpoint × status). At 5KB/series = 500MB just for that ONE metric. Add 10 more metrics with user_id = 5GB RAM gone. Prometheus OOM-kills because the Go runtime can't allocate more memory. The fix: metric_relabel_configs with action: labeldrop to strip dangerous labels BEFORE they enter the TSDB. Or better: never instrument with unbounded labels in the first place.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cardinality Explosion — Before and After
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  BEFORE (cardinality explosion):                                   │
│    metric: http_requests_total                                     │
│    labels: {user_id, endpoint, method, status}                     │
│    user_id = 100,000 unique values                                 │
│    100,000 × 5 endpoints × 4 methods × 5 statuses = 10M series!   │
│    10M × 5KB = 50GB RAM → 💀 OOM kill                              │
│                                                                  │
│  AFTER (drop user_id label):                                       │
│    metric_relabel_configs:                                         │
│    - action: labeldrop                                             │
│      regex: user_id                                                │
│    → user_id stripped BEFORE entering TSDB                         │
│    5 endpoints × 4 methods × 5 statuses = 100 series               │
│    100 × 5KB = 500KB RAM → ✅ Healthy                              │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Cardinality explosion = #1 Prometheus failure mode             │
│  💡 Fix: metric_relabel_configs + labeldrop                       │
│  💡 Better: never instrument with unbounded labels                │
│  💡 Labels should answer WHERE, not WHO                            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Cardinality is the most dangerous Prometheus anti-pattern. The PCA exam tests your ability to identify dangerous labels and fix cardinality explosions with metric_relabel_configs.

Fundamentals Q67 of 209
Does Prometheus downsample data natively? If not, how is downsampling achieved for long-term storage?
A) Yes — Prometheus automatically downsamples data older than 7 days to 5-minute resolution
B) Downsampling is not needed — Prometheus stores raw data forever at full resolution
C) No — Prometheus stores raw data at scrape resolution and does NOT downsample natively. Downsampling is done by external tools: Thanos Compactor (creates 5m and 1h downsampled blocks from raw data in S3) or Cortex/Mimir (built-in downsampling). Without downsampling, querying years of raw data is extremely slow and expensive
D) Downsampling is configured via --storage.tsdb.downsample.interval flag in Prometheus
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus stores data at the scrape resolution — if you scrape every 15s, every sample is kept for the retention period. There is no built-in downsampling. For long-term storage (years), storing every 15s sample is wasteful: a metric scraped every 15s produces ~2 million samples/year. Downsampling via Thanos Compactor creates aggregated blocks at 5-minute and 1-hour resolutions from the raw data, drastically reducing storage and query time for historical data. For anihpj: Prometheus keeps 30 days of raw 15s data locally; Thanos keeps 5m downsampled data for 6 months and 1h for 2 years in S3.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Downsampling — Thanos Compactor
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  RAW DATA (Prometheus, 15s scrape):                                │
│    Block 0-2h:    480 samples per series (every 15s)              │
│    Block 2h-4h:   480 samples                                     │
│    ...30 days: ~86,400 samples per series                          │
│                                                                  │
│  THANOS COMPACTOR DOWNSAMPLING:                                    │
│    Raw → 5m resolution: avg/min/max/count per 5-minute window    │
│    → Storage reduction: ~20× (480 samples → 24 aggregates)       │
│    → Query speed: ~20× faster for range queries                  │
│                                                                  │
│    Raw → 1h resolution: avg/min/max/count per 1-hour window      │
│    → Storage reduction: ~240×                                     │
│    → Good enough for multi-year trend analysis                   │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus: NO native downsampling                            │
│  💡 Thanos Compactor: handles downsampling in object storage     │
│  💡 Cortex/Mimir: built-in downsampling as part of the system    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Know that Prometheus does NOT downsample natively, and that Thanos Compactor (or Cortex/Mimir) handles this for long-term storage.

Fundamentals Q68 of 209
Does Prometheus have built-in authentication? How do you secure access to the Prometheus UI and API?
A) Yes — Prometheus has a built-in login page. Configure users in prometheus.yml under auth
B) No — Prometheus has ZERO built-in authentication. Secure it by placing a reverse proxy (nginx, Apache) with basic auth in front, or use oauth2-proxy for OIDC/OAuth2 SSO. For K8s, use NetworkPolicy to restrict access + kubectl port-forward for admin access
C) Prometheus uses Kubernetes RBAC for authentication — it's built into the K8s integration
D) Prometheus generates a random admin password on first startup — find it in /var/log/prometheus.log
✅ Correct Answer: B

Prometheus has no built-in authentication, authorization, or encryption — by design. The philosophy: Prometheus is a component, not a platform, and security is handled by the infrastructure layer. Solutions: (1) Reverse proxy (nginx with basic_auth or oauth2-proxy with Google/GitHub OAuth) in front of Prometheus, (2) Kubernetes NetworkPolicy to restrict which pods can reach Prometheus, (3) kubectl port-forward for temporary admin access without exposing Prometheus externally. For anihpj: in production, Prometheus is not exposed to the internet — access is via Grafana (which HAS auth) querying Prometheus internally, or via kubectl port-forward for admin tasks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Securing Prometheus — No Built-in Auth
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PROMETHEUS (no auth, no TLS, no encryption):                     │
│                                                                  │
│  OPTION 1: Reverse Proxy (nginx)                                  │
│    User → nginx (HTTPS + basic_auth) → Prometheus (HTTP, local) │
│    ✅ Simple, well-understood                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  OPTION 2: OAuth2 Proxy                                           │
│    User → oauth2-proxy (Google/GitHub SSO) → Prometheus          │
│    ✅ SSO, no shared passwords                                    │
│                                                                  │
│  OPTION 3: K8s NetworkPolicy + port-forward                       │
│    Prometheus only reachable from Grafana + within cluster        │
│    Admin: kubectl port-forward svc/prometheus 9090:9090           │
│    ✅ Most secure (no external exposure)                          │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus intentionally has NO built-in auth                 │
│  💡 Use infrastructure layer (proxy, NetworkPolicy, VPN)         │
│  💡 Grafana has auth — users access metrics THERE, not directly  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Prometheus's lack of built-in auth is a well-known design choice. The PCA exam tests that you know this and how to secure it externally.

Fundamentals Q69 of 209
What is the practical limit for active time series on a single Prometheus instance, and what hardware factors influence it?
A) 1,000 active series — Prometheus is designed for very small deployments only
B) There is no practical limit — Prometheus scales to billions of series on a single machine
C) 100,000 active series — limited by the Go garbage collector
D) ~10 million active series on high-end hardware (64-128GB RAM, fast SSD). Influenced by: RAM (each series ~3-5KB), CPU (PromQL queries, compaction), disk IOPS (WAL writes, compaction), and scrape volume (samples/sec). 1M samples/sec is a reasonable upper bound for a single instance
✅ Correct Answer: D

The practical limit for a single Prometheus instance is ~10 million active series, ~1 million samples/second ingestion, on hardware with 64-128GB RAM and fast NVMe SSD. Key hardware factors: RAM — each active series in the head block uses ~3-5KB (10M × 4KB = 40GB for series alone, plus WAL, query buffers, Go runtime). CPU — PromQL queries scan series, compaction merges blocks, both CPU-intensive. Disk IOPS — WAL writes every scrape, compaction reads/writes blocks. Network — scraping 1000 targets at 15s = 66 req/sec, manageable. For anihpj (thousands of series, not millions), resource usage is minimal — a 2GB/1CPU Prometheus instance is more than sufficient.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Sizing — Hardware Requirements
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SCALE                 SERIES    SAMPLES/S  RAM    CPU   DISK    │
│  ────────────────────  ────────  ─────────  ─────  ────  ────── │
│  Tiny (dev, anihpj)    < 50K     < 5K       2GB    1 vCPU 20GB  │
│  Small                 50-200K   5-20K      4GB    2 vCPU 50GB  │
│  Medium                200K-1M   20-100K    8GB    4 vCPU 200GB │
│  Large                 1-5M      100-500K   16GB   8 vCPU 500GB │
│  XL                    5-10M     0.5-1M     32GB   16 vCPU 1TB │
│  XXL (max single)      ~10M      ~1M        64GB   32 vCPU 2TB │
│  Beyond → shard or use Thanos/Cortex/Mimir                       │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 anihpj: Tiny tier — 2GB RAM, 1 CPU, 20GB disk is plenty     │
│  💡 Rule of thumb: 1M series needs ~4GB RAM + query overhead     │
│  💡 Disk = (samples/sec × bytes/sample × retention_seconds)      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests Prometheus sizing and limits. Know the ~10M series practical limit, key hardware factors (RAM most critical), and when to move to Thanos/Cortex.

Fundamentals Q70 of 209
Can Prometheus run as a clustered, distributed system natively? How is clustering achieved?
A) No — Prometheus has NO native clustering, sharding, or distributed consensus. "Clustering" is achieved by running multiple independent instances (each scraping all or a subset of targets) + an aggregation layer (Thanos Query, Grafana) to provide a unified view. This is by design — simplicity over complexity
B) Yes — set --cluster.enabled=true and Prometheus forms a RAFT cluster automatically
C) Yes — Prometheus uses etcd for cluster membership and leader election
D) Prometheus automatically clusters when deployed as a Kubernetes StatefulSet with 3+ replicas
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus explicitly chose simplicity over distributed complexity. There is no native clustering, no RAFT, no sharding, no leader election. Each Prometheus instance is a completely independent, self-contained monitoring server. "Clustering" is achieved architecturally by: (1) running 2+ identical Prometheus instances for HA (each scrapes everything), (2) sharding targets across instances (each scrapes a subset via hashmod relabeling), (3) using Thanos/Cortex/Mimir as a distributed aggregation layer, (4) using Grafana with multiple Prometheus datasources. This philosophy is fundamental to Prometheus's design — it's a feature, not a bug.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus "Clustering" — Architecture, Not Built-in
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  NATIVE: NO clustering, NO sharding, NO consensus                │
│                                                                  │
│  ARCHITECTURAL PATTERNS FOR SCALE:                                │
│                                                                  │
│  1. HA (redundancy):                                              │
│     Prom A scrapes all targets                                    │
│     Prom B scrapes all targets (identical config)                 │
│     Thanos Query deduplicates                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  2. Sharding (scale targets):                                     │
│     Prom 1 scrapes targets hashmod 0 of 3                         │
│     Prom 2 scrapes targets hashmod 1 of 3                         │
│     Prom 3 scrapes targets hashmod 2 of 3                         │
│     Thanos Query merges all                                       │
│                                                                  │
│  3. Federation (hierarchical):                                    │
│     Regional Prom → Global Prom (aggregated metrics only)         │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 "Simplicity over complexity" — core Prometheus philosophy    │
│  💡 Single binary, single responsibility, easy to understand     │
│  💡 For actual distributed clustering: use Thanos/Cortex/Mimir   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: "Prometheus does not cluster" is a fundamental design principle. The PCA exam tests this — know that HA = running independent instances, not a cluster.

Fundamentals Q71 of 209
Why is Pushgateway not suitable for monitoring long-running services, and what is the correct pattern instead?
A) Pushgateway is perfect for all services — it's the recommended monitoring pattern
B) Pushgateway can't handle HTTP traffic — it only supports gRPC
C) Pushgateway has: no TTL (stale data), no HA (SPOF), can't track instance-level metrics (multiple instances push conflicting values), and no scrape-based UP/DOWN detection. For long-running services, the correct pattern is: expose a /metrics endpoint and let Prometheus scrape it via Pull model
D) Pushgateway is deprecated — it's been removed from Prometheus 3.x
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Pushgateway is a special-purpose tool for batch jobs only. Using it for long-running services (web servers, APIs) is an anti-pattern because: (1) metrics never expire (no TTL) — if a service stops pushing, old metrics persist forever as false positives, (2) multiple instances of the same service push conflicting values (which instance's value is correct?), (3) you lose scrape-based health detection (Prometheus can't tell if the service is UP/DOWN), (4) single point of failure. The correct pattern for long-running services: expose a /metrics endpoint and let Prometheus scrape it — this gives you automatic UP/DOWN detection, no stale data, and per-instance metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pushgateway Anti-Pattern — Don't Use for Services
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ❌ WRONG: Using Pushgateway for long-running services             │
│                                                                  │
│  anihpj-api-1 ──push──┐                                          │
│  anihpj-api-2 ──push──┤──→ Pushgateway ←──scrape── Prometheus    │
│  anihpj-api-3 ──push──┘   (SPOF, no TTL, conflicting values)     │
│                                                                  │
│  Problems:                                                        │
│    ❌ If api-1 crashes, its metrics persist forever               │
│    ❌ api-1 and api-2 both push http_requests_total → conflict   │
│    ❌ Prometheus can't detect api-1 is DOWN                       │
│    ❌ Pushgateway crashes → all monitoring stops                  │
│                                                                  │
│  ✅ CORRECT: Pull model for services                              │
│                                                                  │
│  Prometheus ──GET /metrics──→ anihpj-api-1:8000                  │
│            ──GET /metrics──→ anihpj-api-2:8000                  │
│            ──GET /metrics──→ anihpj-api-3:8000                  │
│                                                                  │
│  Benefits:                                                        │
│    ✅ Pod dies → scrape fails → target DOWN → alert              │
│    ✅ Each instance has its own metrics (instance label)         │
│    ✅ No SPOF (Prometheus scrapes each pod directly)             │
│    ✅ Metrics automatically TTL when pod is gone                 │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Pushgateway = batch jobs ONLY (cronjobs, one-shot processes) │
│  💡 Long-running services = always Pull (expose /metrics)        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: This is one of the most tested Pushgateway concepts. The PCA exam will present a web server scenario and ask if Pushgateway is appropriate — the answer is NO. Pushgateway = batch jobs only.

2.4 Data Model & LabelsQ72-Q79

Fundamentals Q72 of 209
In Prometheus's data model, what uniquely identifies a time series?
A) The combination of metric NAME and LABELS (key-value pairs). e.g., http_requests_total{method="GET", status="200", endpoint="/api/jobs"} is ONE time series. Change any label value → different time series
B) Just the metric name — labels are optional metadata that don't affect series identity
C) The metric name plus a UUID that Prometheus generates internally for each series
D) The target instance that produced the metric — each target has its own series namespace
✅ Correct Answer: A

A time series is uniquely identified by the combination of metric name + all label key-value pairs. For example: http_requests_total{method="GET", status="200", endpoint="/api/jobs"} is one series; http_requests_total{method="POST", status="200", endpoint="/api/jobs"} is a DIFFERENT series (different method label); http_requests_total{method="GET", status="200", endpoint="/api/users"} is yet another (different endpoint). This is why cardinality matters: each new combination of label values creates a brand new time series consuming memory and disk. For anihpj: 4 methods × 5 statuses × 10 endpoints = 200 series just for http_requests_total.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Time Series Identity — Metric Name + Labels
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ONE METRIC → MANY TIME SERIES:                                   │
│                                                                  │
│  Metric: http_requests_total                                      │
│                                                                  │
│  Series 1: {method="GET",  status="200", endpoint="/api/jobs"}   │
│  Series 2: {method="GET",  status="404", endpoint="/api/jobs"}   │
│  Series 3: {method="POST", status="200", endpoint="/api/jobs"}   │
│  Series 4: {method="GET",  status="200", endpoint="/api/users"}  │
│  ...                                                              │
│                                                                  │
│  Each series = independent time series:                           │
│    Series 1: [(t1, 100), (t2, 150), (t3, 200), ...]             │
│    Series 2: [(t1, 5),   (t2, 3),   (t3, 7),   ...]            │
│    Series 3: [(t1, 20),  (t2, 25),  (t3, 30),  ...]            │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 metric_name{labels} = unique time series identity            │
│  💡 Adding a new label value = creating new series (cardinality)│
│  💡 __name__ is itself a label: {__name__="http_requests_total"} │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding time series identity is fundamental to Prometheus. Know: metric name + labels = unique series, and why this makes cardinality control essential.

Fundamentals Q73 of 209
What are the Prometheus label naming best practices? How should anihpj/jobpost metrics be labeled?
A) Use CamelCase with descriptive sentences as label values — e.g., {UserID="12345", RequestPath="/api/Jobs/Apply"}
B) Labels should be lowercase with snake_case. Use semantic, bounded-value labels: job, instance, method, status, endpoint, version. Avoid unbounded labels: user_id, email, IP, request_id, session_id. anihpj example: http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api", method="get", status="200", endpoint="jobs_list"}
C) Labels are free-form — any naming convention works as long as you're consistent
D) Labels must use only uppercase letters and numbers — lowercase is not supported by PromQL
✅ Correct Answer: B

Prometheus label naming conventions: lowercase, snake_case (e.g., http_method, not HTTPMethod or http-method). Labels should be semantic (describe the dimension: what, where, how) and bounded (limited number of unique values — <100). Never use labels for: user IDs, emails, IPs, request IDs, session IDs, timestamps, or any value that can grow unbounded. For anihpj: {job="anihpj-api", method="get", status="200", endpoint="jobs_list", version="v2.3.1"} — all bounded, all lowercase, all semantic dimensions that help you slice and aggregate metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Label Naming — Dos and Don'ts for anihpj
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✅ DO (bounded, semantic, lowercase):                             │
│    job="anihpj-api"           ← which service?                    │
│    method="get"               ← GET, POST, PUT, DELETE (4 values)│
│    status="200"               ← 2xx, 3xx, 4xx, 5xx (~20 values) │
│    endpoint="jobs_list"       ← API endpoints (~10-20 values)    │
│    version="v2.3.1"           ← deployed version (~5-10 values)  │
│    le="0.5"                   ← histogram bucket (configurable)  │
│                                                                  │
│  ❌ DON'T (unbounded, PII, explode cardinality):                 │
│    user_id="12345"            ← 100,000+ values → 💥             │
│    email="user@example.com"   ← PII + unbounded → 💥             │
│    request_id="a1b2c3d4"      ← unique per request → 💥💥💥     │
│    ip_address="10.244.1.23"   ← unlimited values → 💥            │
│    timestamp="1718387535"     ← changes every scrape → 💥       │
│    session_id="xyz789"        ← unique per session → 💥          │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Labels = WHERE dimensions (bounded < 100 values)             │
│  💡 If it can be >100 unique values, it's NOT a label            │
│  💡 User IDs, emails, request IDs → put in LOGS, not labels     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Label naming is a PCA topic. Know: lowercase, snake_case, semantic, bounded (<100 values). Never use unbounded identifiers as labels.

Fundamentals Q74 of 209
What are __ (double underscore) labels in Prometheus, and what is their purpose?
A) Double underscore labels are user-defined labels that start with __ — they're treated as private/internal
B) __ labels are deprecated — they were used in Prometheus 1.x and removed in 2.x
C) __ labels are internal/transient labels used during the relabeling process. Examples: __name__ (metric name), __address__ (target host:port), __scheme__, __metrics_path__, __meta_kubernetes_* (K8s SD metadata). They are automatically removed after relabeling completes — they never appear in stored time series
D) __ labels indicate metrics that are "hidden" from PromQL queries but still stored
✅ Correct Answer: C

Labels starting with __ (double underscore) are internal labels used during the relabeling pipeline. They exist temporarily to carry metadata and configuration between SD discovery and the final scrape. The most important ones: __name__ (metric name), __address__ (target host:port — what Prometheus actually connects to), __scheme__ (http/https), __metrics_path__ (URL path, default /metrics), __meta_* (service discovery metadata like pod labels, namespace, annotations). After all relabel_configs are applied, any remaining labels starting with __ are automatically dropped — they never appear in stored time series.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Double Underscore Labels — Internal Use Only
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  RELABELING PIPELINE (__ labels exist here):                      │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 1: Service Discovery produces:                             │
│    __address__: "10.244.1.23:8000"                               │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app: "anihpj-api"                 │
│    __meta_kubernetes_namespace: "anihpj"                         │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 2: relabel_configs transforms:                              │
│    - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app]            │
│      target_label: app                                            │
│    → __meta label → regular label "app"                          │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 3: After relabeling, all remaining __ labels are DROPPED:  │
│    Final labels: {job="anihpj-api", app="anihpj-api", ...}       │
│    (no __ labels in stored time series)                          │
│                                                                  │
│  IMPORTANT __ LABELS:                                             │
│    __name__            metric name                                │
│    __address__         target host:port (where Prometheus connects)│
│    __scheme__           http or https                             │
│    __metrics_path__    URL path (default: /metrics)               │
│    __param_      URL parameters                             │
│    __meta_*            service discovery metadata                 │
│    __tmp_*             temporary labels for relabeling            │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 __ = internal, transient, auto-deleted after relabeling      │
│  💡 Use __meta_* in relabel_configs, never in final labels       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Double underscore labels are essential for understanding relabel_configs. Know the key __ labels (__address__, __name__, __meta_*) and that they're auto-removed after relabeling.

Fundamentals Q75 of 209
What are the two reserved labels that every Prometheus target MUST have, and how are they set?
A) name and namespace — they are set from Kubernetes metadata during service discovery
B) job (set from scrape_config's job_name) and instance (set to the target's __address__ — host:port). Every scraped time series automatically gets these two labels. They are the minimum required labels for any target
C) severity and priority — they are set from alerting rule annotations
D) env and region — they must be set manually in external_labels or relabel_configs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Every Prometheus target automatically gets two reserved labels: job (the job_name from the scrape_config — e.g., "anihpj-api") and instance (the __address__ of the target after relabeling — e.g., "10.244.1.23:8000"). These are the minimum identification for any time series. The job label groups targets by their scrape configuration (one job = one scrape_config entry). The instance label uniquely identifies each target within a job. Together, {job, instance} should uniquely identify every scrape target. For anihpj: {job="anihpj-api", instance="10.244.1.23:8000"} uniquely identifies one API pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
job and instance — The Two Reserved Labels
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  scrape_configs:                                                   │
│    - job_name: 'anihpj-api'         ← becomes job label           │
│      kubernetes_sd_configs:                                        │
│      - role: pod                                                  │
│                                                                  │
│  After discovery + relabeling, each target gets:                   │
│                                                                  │
│  Target 1:                                                         │
│    job: "anihpj-api"                                              │
│    instance: "10.244.1.23:8000"                                   │
│    → Every scraped metric from this pod has these labels          │
│                                                                  │
│  Target 2:                                                         │
│    job: "anihpj-api"                                              │
│    instance: "10.244.1.24:8000"                                   │
│                                                                  │
│  In expressions:                                                   │
│    up{job="anihpj-api"}  → health of ALL API pods                 │
│    up{instance="10.244.1.23:8000"} → health of specific pod       │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 job = which scrape_config (logical group)                     │
│  💡 instance = which specific target (host:port)                  │
│  💡 Every target MUST have both — set automatically               │
│  💡 {job, instance} should be unique per target                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: job and instance are the two fundamental labels in Prometheus. Know that they're set automatically and that {job, instance} uniquely identifies every scraped target.

Fundamentals Q76 of 209
What is the difference between instrumentation labels (set by the application) and target labels (set by Prometheus during scraping)?
A) There is no difference — all labels come from the application's /metrics endpoint
B) Instrumentation labels are in the metric name; target labels are in the metric value
C) Instrumentation labels are set by Grafana; target labels are set by Prometheus
D) Instrumentation labels are set IN the application code (e.g., method="GET", status="200"). Target labels are ADDED by Prometheus during scraping (job, instance) or via relabel_configs (namespace, env, region). Both appear on the final time series — they are merged during scraping
✅ Correct Answer: D

Labels come from two sources: Instrumentation labels are embedded in the application code by developers — e.g., http_requests_total{method="GET", status="200"} is what Django exposes at /metrics. Target labels are added by Prometheus during or after scraping — job and instance are always added, and relabel_configs can add more (namespace, env, region from K8s SD metadata). Both sets are merged into the final time series. For anihpj: Django instruments {method, status, endpoint}; Prometheus adds {job, instance, namespace} via K8s SD and relabeling. The final series has ALL these labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Instrumentation Labels vs Target Labels
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  STEP 1: Application (Django) exposes /metrics:                   │
│    http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200"} 54321          │
│    ↑ INSTRUMENTATION LABELS (set by app developer)               │
│                                                                  │
│  STEP 2: Prometheus scrapes and ADDS target labels:               │
│    job="anihpj-api"           ← from scrape_config job_name      │
│    instance="10.244.1.23:8000" ← from __address__                 │
│                                                                  │
│  STEP 3: relabel_configs may ADD more:                            │
│    namespace="anihpj"         ← from K8s SD __meta_ labels       │
│    version="v2.3.1"           ← from pod label                   │
│                                                                  │
│  FINAL TIME SERIES (merged):                                      │
│    http_requests_total{                                            │
│      method="GET",                                                │
│      status="200",             ← instrumentation                 │
│      job="anihpj-api",                                            │
│      instance="10.244.1.23:8000", ← target                       │
│      namespace="anihpj",                                          │
│      version="v2.3.1"          ← relabeling                      │
│    } 54321                                                        │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Instrumentation = what the app knows about itself             │
│  💡 Target = what Prometheus knows about the app                  │
│  💡 Both are merged — all labels are equal in PromQL             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding the two sources of labels helps you debug "where did this label come from?" Know: instrumentation = app code, target = Prometheus config + SD metadata.

Fundamentals Q77 of 209
How should anihpj label its metrics for effective querying and alerting?
A) Use as many labels as possible — more labels = more query flexibility
B) Use MEANINGFUL, BOUNDED labels: method (GET/POST/PUT/DELETE), status (2xx/4xx/5xx), endpoint (/api/jobs, /api/search), category (tech/health/finance). Each label should: (1) represent an independent dimension, (2) have bounded cardinality (<100 values), (3) be needed for query grouping/filtering. Do NOT label with user_id, email, request_id, or any unbounded identifier. Labels are for AGGREGATION — ask "will I group by this?" before adding
C) Use only the default job and instance labels — custom labels add complexity
D) Put all context in the metric name: anihpj_api_GET_200_jobs_list_requests_total
✅ Correct Answer: B

Labeling rule: "Would I GROUP BY or FILTER BY this label in a query?" If yes → label. If no → don't. Labels define the DIMENSIONS of your metrics. For anihpj: `method` and `status` are labels because you query "error rate by endpoint" and "requests by method." `user_id` is NOT a label because you'd never aggregate by individual user in Prometheus.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Label Design — The Aggregation Test
THE GOLDEN RULE: "Will I aggregate/group/filter by this?"

✅ GOOD LABELS (pass the test):
  method    → "Show me request rate by method" ✅
  status    → "Filter to only 5xx errors" ✅
  endpoint  → "P99 latency per endpoint" ✅
  category  → "Jobs posted by category" ✅

❌ BAD LABELS (fail the test):
  user_id   → "Rate per user?" → 100K series, NEVER aggregate by user_id ❌
  email     → "Filter by email?" → cardinality explosion ❌
  ip_addr   → "Group by IP?" → meaningless aggregation ❌
  request_id → "Filter by UUID?" → unique per request, 1:1 with series ❌

ANIHJP LABEL DESIGN:
  http_requests_total{method, status, endpoint, category}
  → method(5) × status(5) × endpoint(30) × category(10) = 7,500 series max
  → ALL labels pass the aggregation test
  → Bounded cardinality → safe for Prometheus

💡 Ask "will I GROUP BY this?" before adding any label
💡 Labels = aggregation dimensions (NOT metadata)
💡 Bounded cardinality = predictable resource usage

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Label design is a core Prometheus skill. The "aggregation test" determines good vs bad labels. PCA tests this.

Fundamentals Q78 of 209
How do you manage label cardinality in a growing anihpj deployment with 50+ API endpoints and multiple environments?
A) Cardinality isn't an issue until you reach 1M+ series — no management needed for small deployments
B) Use the same metric name with all endpoints in a single comma-separated label value to reduce series
C) (1) Use `metric_relabel_configs` with `action: drop` to remove high-cardinality labels at scrape time. (2) Use `action: labeldrop` to strip unnecessary labels from targets. (3) Group endpoints by pattern (keep `endpoint` but limit to ~30 most critical; group rest as `other`). (4) Use separate Prometheus instances per environment (prod/staging) rather than one giant instance. (5) Aggregate in code before exposing: `job_posts_by_category` not `job_posts_by_id`
D) Cardinality is only a storage problem — use larger disks and it's solved
✅ Correct Answer: C

Cardinality management = defense in depth. Layer 1: don't create high-cardinality labels (code discipline). Layer 2: drop at scrape time (relabel_configs). Layer 3: aggregate in application code. Layer 4: separate Prometheus instances. For anihpj growing from 10 to 50+ endpoints: endpoint cardinality 50 × method 5 × status 5 = 1,250 series per metric — still fine. The risk is adding `user_id` or `session_id` accidentally.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cardinality Management — Defense in Depth
LAYER 1: Code discipline (PREVENT)
  ✅ http_requests_total{method, status, endpoint}  — bounded
  ❌ http_requests_total{user_id}                    — unbounded!

LAYER 2: Scrape-time drop (PROTECT)
  metric_relabel_configs:
    - source_labels: [user_id]
      regex: '.+'
      action: drop               # remove before storage
    - regex: '(endpoint|method|status|job|instance)'
      action: labelkeep          # keep ONLY approved labels

LAYER 3: Application aggregation (REDUCE)
  Instead of: job_post_views_total{job_id}  → 100K series
  Use:        job_post_views_total{category} → 10 series

LAYER 4: Separate instances (ISOLATE)
  Prometheus-prod → production metrics only
  Prometheus-staging → staging metrics only
  → Each handles manageable cardinality independently

CARDINALITY MATH FOR ANIHJP:
  endpoint(50) × method(5) × status(5) × instance(5) = 6,250 series
  Add `category(10)`: 62,500 series  ← STILL FINE
  Add `user_id(100K)`: 625M series    ← OOM! ☠️

💡 Defense in depth: prevent → protect → reduce → isolate
💡 relabel_configs is your safety net at scrape time
💡 Monitor: count({__name__=~".+"}) → total series count

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Cardinality management strategy is a PCA topic. Know the four layers: code discipline, relabel drop, app aggregation, separate instances.

Fundamentals Q79 of 209
What are external labels in Prometheus and how should you configure them for anihpj's multi-environment setup?
A) External labels are GLOBAL labels added to ALL time series by a Prometheus instance via `global.external_labels` in prometheus.yml. Typical: `{cluster="anihpj-prod", region="us-east-1", env="production"}`. They identify WHICH Prometheus instance the data came from — critical for federation, Thanos/Cortex, and multi-cluster setups. For anihpj: each environment (prod/staging/dev) has its own Prometheus with unique external_labels so metrics from different clusters never conflict
B) External labels are labels added by external sources like Alertmanager — not Prometheus configuration
C) External labels replace all internal labels — they're a clean-slate labeling approach
D) External labels are only used by Grafana for dashboard filtering — they have no effect on Prometheus storage
✅ Correct Answer: A

External labels = Prometheus instance identity. They're added at scrape time to EVERY series. Critical for: (1) Federation — `match[]` queries can filter by external label, (2) Deduplication — Thanos/Cortex use them to identify which Prometheus sent data, (3) Multi-cluster dashboards — Grafana filters by cluster label. For anihpj: `cluster`, `env`, `region` as external labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
External Labels — Multi-Environment Identity
# prometheus-prod.yml
global:
  external_labels:
    cluster: anihpj-prod
    env: production
    region: us-east-1
    team: backend

# prometheus-staging.yml
global:
  external_labels:
    cluster: anihpj-staging
    env: staging
    region: us-east-1
    team: backend

EFFECT ON METRICS:
  http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api"}
  → http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api",cluster="anihpj-prod",env="production",...}
  External labels are ADDED to every scraped series automatically

FEDERATION USE:
  # Central Prometheus federates from all clusters:
  scrape_configs:
    - job_name: 'federate-prod'
      honor_labels: true          # external labels take precedence
      params:
        'match[]': ['{__name__=~".+"}']  # all metrics from prod

MULTI-CLUSTER DASHBOARD:
  Grafana: rate(http_requests_total{env="production"}[5m])
  vs
  Grafana: rate(http_requests_total{env="staging"}[5m])
  → Same metric name, distinguished by external label!

💡 External labels = Prometheus instance IDENTITY
💡 Added to ALL series at scrape time
💡 Essential for: federation, Thanos/Cortex, multi-cluster
💡 honor_labels: true → external labels override target labels

Why this matters for the PCA exam: External labels are essential for multi-environment Prometheus. PCA tests understanding of their role in federation.

2.5 Exposition FormatQ80-Q85

Fundamentals Q80 of 209
What is the Prometheus exposition format, and what does a healthy /metrics response look like?
A) JSON format with metric arrays — {metrics: [{name: "cpu", value: 45}]}
B) XML format with <metric> tags
C) Plain text with HELP (description), TYPE (counter/gauge/histogram/summary), and metric lines (name{labels} value). Example: # HELP http_requests_total ...\n# TYPE http_requests_total counter\nhttp_requests_total{status="200"} 54321
D) Binary protobuf format — not human-readable
✅ Correct Answer: C

The exposition format has 3 line types: HELP, TYPE, and metric lines. Histograms add _bucket/_sum/_count; summaries add quantile lines + _sum/_count. For anihpj: each /metrics endpoint returns this text/plain format with all Django + business metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exposition Format — anihpj /metrics Output
# HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests
# TYPE http_requests_total counter
http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200"} 54321
http_requests_total{method="POST",status="201"} 8765
# HELP active_users Current active users
# TYPE active_users gauge
active_users 47
# HELP api_duration_seconds API request duration
# TYPE api_duration_seconds histogram
api_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.5"} 43210
api_duration_seconds_bucket{le="+Inf"} 54321
api_duration_seconds_sum 23456.78
api_duration_seconds_count 54321

Why this matters for the PCA exam: You must recognize HELP, TYPE, and metric lines. Know that histograms expose _bucket/_sum/_count and summaries expose quantile + _sum/_count.

Fundamentals Q81 of 209
What is OpenMetrics and how does it relate to the Prometheus exposition format?
A) OpenMetrics is a competing format from Google — Prometheus doesn't support it
B) OpenMetrics is the IETF-standardized evolution of Prometheus format, adding UNIT lines, mandatory timestamps, and stricter parsing. They are 99% compatible — Prometheus scrapes both
C) OpenMetrics is a JSON-based format that replaces the Prometheus text format entirely in v3.0
D) OpenMetrics is the same as Prometheus format — just renamed for marketing
✅ Correct Answer: B

OpenMetrics adds # UNIT lines, mandatory timestamps, and a formal IETF spec. Prometheus scrapes both formats transparently. It's the future direction for metric exposition.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus vs OpenMetrics — Key Differences
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PROMETHEUS FORMAT (original):                               │
│                                                              │
│  # HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests              │
│  # TYPE http_requests_total counter                          │
│  http_requests_total{method="GET"} 54321                     │
│                                                              │
│  OPENMETRICS FORMAT (IETF-standardized):                     │
│                                                              │
│  # HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests              │
│  # TYPE http_requests_total counter                          │
│  # UNIT http_requests_total requests    ← NEW: unit suffix   │
│  http_requests_total{method="GET"} 54321 1718387535          │
│                                          ↑ NEW: timestamp    │
│  # EOF                                 ← NEW: end marker     │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                              │
│  KEY ADDITIONS:                                              │
│    # UNIT  → specifies the unit (seconds, bytes, requests)   │
│    timestamp → mandatory Unix timestamp per sample           │
│    # EOF     → marks end of exposition stream                │
│                                                              │
│  COMPATIBILITY: 99% — Prometheus scrapes BOTH formats        │
│  Prometheus format is a SUBSET of OpenMetrics                │
│                                                              │
│  anihpj /metrics: currently Prometheus format               │
│  Future: migrate to OpenMetrics for standard compliance      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: OpenMetrics is the modern standard. Know it adds # UNIT, mandatory timestamps, and # EOF. Prometheus scrapes both formats transparently — OpenMetrics is the IETF-ratified evolution of the Prometheus exposition format.

Fundamentals Q82 of 209
What are the 5 valid TYPE values in the exposition format?
A) counter, gauge, histogram, summary, untyped — each has specific semantics that affect which PromQL functions work
B) integer, float, string, boolean, array — standard data types
C) metric, event, trace, log, span — observability signal types
D) pull, push, hybrid, static, dynamic — collection method types
✅ Correct Answer: A

The 5 types: counter (only increases, rate()/increase() work), gauge (up/down), histogram (_bucket/_sum/_count), summary (quantile lines + _sum/_count), untyped (unknown). The TYPE determines which PromQL functions apply.

📝 Detailed Explanation
5 Valid TYPE Values
counter   → rate(), increase(), irate()
gauge     → direct use, delta(), deriv()
histogram → histogram_quantile()
summary   → pre-calculated quantiles (client-side)
untyped   → treated as gauge (fallback)

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Know all 5 types and which PromQL functions work with each. rate() only works on counters.

Fundamentals Q83 of 209
How are histogram metrics represented in the exposition format?
A) A single line with all bucket counts comma-separated
B) JSON embedded in the text format
C) Separate _bucket lines for each bucket plus _total instead of _count
D) _bucket{le="boundary"} for each cumulative bucket (including +Inf), _sum for total of all values, _count for total observations. All three are required
✅ Correct Answer: D

A histogram has 3 metric families: _bucket (cumulative, one per boundary + +Inf), _sum, _count. Buckets are cumulative — each includes all smaller observations. +Inf bucket is mandatory and equals _count.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Histogram Exposition Format
api_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.1"}  12453  ← cumulative
api_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.5"}  43210
api_duration_seconds_bucket{le="+Inf"} 54321  ← mandatory, = _count
api_duration_seconds_sum   23456.78
api_duration_seconds_count 54321

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Histogram format is a PCA topic. Know the 3 families, that buckets are cumulative, and +Inf is mandatory.

Fundamentals Q84 of 209
How are summary metrics different from histograms in the exposition format?
A) Summaries use quantile lines ({quantile="0.5"} val) instead of bucket lines ({le="0.5"} count). Quantiles calculated client-side, NOT aggregatable across instances
B) Summaries and histograms look identical in the format — only the TYPE line differs
C) Summaries don't have _sum or _count — only quantile lines
D) Summaries use bucket lines with qt= instead of le=
✅ Correct Answer: A

Summaries expose {quantile="0.5"} values (client-calculated) + _sum + _count. Histograms expose {le="boundary"} buckets (server-calculated) + _sum + _count. Summaries cannot be aggregated across instances — use histogram unless you need exact client-side quantiles.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Summary vs Histogram Format
HISTOGRAM:                         SUMMARY:
_bucket{le="0.5"} 43210           {quantile="0.5"} 0.35  ← median
_bucket{le="+Inf"} 54321          {quantile="0.99"} 3.50 ← p99
_sum   23456.78                   _sum   23456.78
_count 54321                      _count 54321
↑ server-side quantiles           ↑ client-side quantiles
✅ aggregatable                   ❌ NOT aggregatable

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Summary vs Histogram is a key distinction. Summaries = client-side quantiles, NOT aggregatable. Use histogram by default.

Fundamentals Q85 of 209
What does a healthy /metrics endpoint look like for anihpj/jobpost?
A) An HTML dashboard page showing all metrics visually
B) A single health score: "anihpj_health 0.95"
C) Plain text with HELP/TYPE for each metric, default process metrics (python_*, process_*), Django metrics (http_requests_total, http_request_duration_seconds), and custom business metrics (job_post_creations_total). Content-Type: text/plain
D) A JSON array of all historical metric values since app startup
✅ Correct Answer: C

A healthy /metrics returns text/plain with: process metrics, Django metrics (django-prometheus), custom business metrics, all with HELP/TYPE lines. If it returns HTML or JSON, the scrape fails.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Healthy anihpj /metrics
$ curl http://anihpj-api:8000/metrics
# HELP python_info Python platform information
# TYPE python_info gauge
python_info{version="3.11.4"} 1
# HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests
# TYPE http_requests_total counter
http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200"} 54321

job_post_creations_total 12453
→ Content-Type: text/plain; HTTP 200
