5.4 Alerting BasicsQ200-Q209

Alerting & Dashboarding Q200 of 209
What are the key alerting best practices from the Google SRE book applied to Prometheus/Alertmanager?
A) (1) Alert on SYMPTOMS, not causes — "P99 latency > 500ms" not "CPU > 80%". (2) Every alert must have a RUNBOOK link. (3) Pages should be ACTIONABLE — if no human action needed, it shouldn't page. (4) Eliminate NOISE: use `for`, grouping, inhibition. (5) Alert on SLO BREACH (error budget burn rate), not individual metric thresholds. (6) Test alerts in CI (promtool test rules). (7) Monitor your monitoring — Prometheus scraping itself, Alertmanager health
B) Alert on EVERY metric anomaly — more alerts = better coverage
C) All alerts should go to all teams for maximum visibility
D) Alerting best practices are from the PCA exam guide, not Google SRE — different frameworks
✅ Correct Answer: A

SRE alerting philosophy: "Alert on symptoms your users care about (latency, errors, availability), not on causes (CPU, memory, disk — unless they directly cause symptoms)." Every page should require human intelligence to respond. If a script could fix it, automate it — don't page. For anihpj: page on "users getting errors" (symptom); don't page on "high CPU" (cause — unless it correlates with user impact).

📝 Detailed Explanation
SRE Alerting Best Practices — Applied to Prometheus
1. ALERT ON SYMPTOMS (not causes):
   ✅ "P99 latency > 500ms" (user-visible symptom)
   ❌ "CPU > 80%" (internal cause — may not affect users)

2. EVERY ALERT HAS A RUNBOOK:
   annotations:
     runbook: "https://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/api-latency"

3. PAGES MUST BE ACTIONABLE:
   ✅ "SLO budget < 10% remaining" → halt deploys, investigate
   ❌ "Deploy happened" → no action needed; use dashboard annotation

4. ELIMINATE NOISE:
   for: 5m          (sustain before alerting)
   group_by: [...]  (bundle related alerts)
   inhibit_rules    (suppress symptoms when root cause fires)

5. ALERT ON BURN RATE (SLO-based):
   burn_rate > 14.4 → page (budget exhausted in 1 hour!)
   burn_rate > 1.0  → ticket (budget exhausted in 30 days)

6. TEST ALERTS IN CI:
   promtool test rules alerts-test.yml → PASS required to merge

7. MONITOR THE MONITORING:
   - Prometheus: up{job="prometheus"} == 0
   - Alertmanager: up{job="alertmanager"} == 0
   - Scrape health: time() - timestamp(up) > 300

💡 Page on: SLO breach, user-visible symptoms
💡 Don't page on: internal causes without user impact
💡 Every page: actionable + runbook + tested

Why this matters for the PCA exam: These SRE alerting best practices are directly tested in PCA. Know: symptom-based alerting, runbooks, actionable pages.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q201 of 209
What is alert fatigue and how do Prometheus + Alertmanager features prevent it?
A) Alert fatigue is when alerts are too quiet — fixed by making all alerts critical severity
B) Alert fatigue is a hardware problem — the server can't process enough alerts per second
C) Alert fatigue only affects large enterprises — small teams don't experience it
D) Alert fatigue = on-call desensitization caused by too many false/unactionable alerts. Prevention: (1) `for` clause prevents transient spikes, (2) alert grouping bundles related alerts into one notification, (3) inhibition suppresses symptoms when root cause fires, (4) silence for planned maintenance, (5) severity routing (critical→page, warning→Slack, info→log). The goal: every page should require human intelligence to respond
✅ Correct Answer: D

Alert fatigue is the #1 alerting failure mode. Prometheus+Alertmanager provide 5 defenses: for (buffer), grouping (bundle), inhibition (root cause only), silence (maintenance), severity (route appropriately). For anihpj: if on-call gets 50 alerts for the same DB issue, they'll ignore ALL alerts within a week. Use all 5 defenses.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Five Defenses Against Alert Fatigue
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. FOR CLAUSE — Buffer against spikes                       │
│     for: 5m → "Only alert if it's sustained, not a blip"     │
│                                                              │
│  2. GROUPING — Bundle related alerts                         │
│     group_by: [alertname, cluster] → "50 pods down" = 1 page │
│                                                              │
│  3. INHIBITION — Suppress symptoms, show root cause          │
│     "DB is down" suppresses "API errors" → 1 page, not 15    │
│                                                              │
│  4. SILENCE — Planned maintenance = no alerts                │
│     2-hour silence during DB migration → expected downtime   │
│                                                              │
│  5. SEVERITY ROUTING — Right channel, right urgency          │
│     critical→PagerDuty, warning→Slack, info→log only         │
│                                                              │
│  RESULT:                                                     │
│    Without defenses: 50+ alerts/day → on-call burns out      │
│    With all 5:       2-3 actionable pages/day → sustainable  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Alert fatigue prevention is a core SRE principle tested in PCA. Know all 5 Prometheus/Alertmanager defenses.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q202 of 209
How do you handle a flapping alert (alternating between firing and resolved every few minutes)?
A) Delete the alert rule — flapping means it's a bad alert
B) (1) Increase `for` duration — if flapping every 2 minutes, set `for: 10m` so it never fires from transient oscillation. (2) Widen the threshold — if alerting at exactly the boundary (e.g., 5% error), hysteresis causes flapping; lower to 4% or raise `for`. (3) Use avg_over_time() to smooth the input signal. (4) Add `keep_firing_for: 5m` (Prometheus 2.42+) to hold alert firing after condition clears. For anihpj: flapping latency alert → widen threshold from 500ms to 750ms with for: 10m
C) Silence the alert permanently — flapping alerts are unfixable
D) Increase scrape interval so the alert fires less frequently
✅ Correct Answer: B

Flapping = oscillation around threshold. Fixes in order: (1) longer `for` (simplest, prevents oscillation from reaching firing state), (2) widen threshold or smooth input with avg_over_time(), (3) add hysteresis via `keep_firing_for`. Never just delete the alert — the underlying condition is real; the threshold just needs tuning.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Fixing a Flapping Alert
FLAPPING ALERT (error rate oscillates 4.8% ↔ 5.2%):
  t=0:00  5.1% → FIRING
  t=0:02  4.9% → RESOLVED
  t=0:04  5.2% → FIRING
  t=0:06  4.7% → RESOLVED
  → 15 alerts/hour = MADNESS! 🤯

FIX 1: Increase `for` (buffer the oscillation)
  for: 2m → for: 10m
  t=0:00  5.1% → PENDING
  t=0:02  4.9% → back to INACTIVE (for never elapsed)
  → 0 alerts! ✅ (threshold crossed but never sustained)

FIX 2: Smooth the input (avg_over_time)
  expr: avg_over_time(error_rate[5m]) > 0.05
  → 5-minute average dampens oscillation → stable signal

FIX 3: Add hysteresis (keep_firing_for)
  keep_firing_for: 5m
  → Alert stays FIRING for 5m after condition clears
  → Prevents rapid fire→resolve→fire cycles

💡 Flapping = threshold too close to normal operating point
💡 Fix hierarchy: for → widen threshold → smooth input → hysteresis
💡 NEVER delete; the condition is real — tune the parameters

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Flapping alert diagnosis and remediation is a practical PCA skill. Know the fix hierarchy.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q203 of 209
What's the difference between alerting on raw metrics vs alerting on SLI burn rate?
A) Raw metric alerting: "error rate > 5% for 5m" — simple, but doesn't account for SLO window. A 5% spike for 5 minutes may be fine if the monthly error budget is 0.1%. Burn rate alerting: "error budget consumption rate > 14.4×" (= all budget consumed in 1 hour) — MULTI-WINDOW, MULTI-BURN-RATE approach from Google SRE. Uses short window (1h) + long window (6h) to detect both fast burns AND slow sustained burns. More sophisticated but fewer false positives
B) Raw metric alerting uses PromQL; burn rate uses Alertmanager expressions — different systems
C) There's no difference — "burn rate" is just marketing terminology for the same thing
D) Burn rate alerting is enterprise-only — requires Google Cloud Monitoring, not Prometheus
✅ Correct Answer: A

Burn rate = "how fast are we consuming error budget?" Multi-window alerting: short window (1h) catches fast burns → page on-call. Long window (6h) catches slow sustained burns → ticket. This is more intelligent than a single threshold because it considers the SLO window context. For anihpj 99.9% SLO: 0.1% error budget. Burn rate 14.4× over 1h = budget exhausted in 1h → page!

📝 Detailed Explanation
Raw Threshold vs Burn Rate Alerting
RAW THRESHOLD (simple, but context-blind):
  rate(errors[5m]) / rate(requests[5m]) > 0.05
  → "Error rate > 5% right NOW"
  → May fire for 5-min spike that uses 0.001% of monthly budget
  → FALSE POSITIVE: page for non-issue

MULTI-WINDOW BURN RATE (SLO-aware, intelligent):
  Short window (1h burn rate > 14.4×):
    → Budget being consumed at 14.4× normal rate
    → All monthly budget exhausted in 1 HOUR
    → PAGE ON-CALL immediately!

  Long window (6h burn rate > 6×):
    → Budget being consumed at 6× normal rate
    → All monthly budget exhausted in 5 HOURS
    → TICKET (not page) — investigate during work hours

BURN RATE PROMQL:
  (1 - SLO) = error budget (0.1% for 99.9%)
  burn_rate = error_rate / (1 - SLO)

  Short window alert:
    sum(rate(errors[1h])) / sum(rate(requests[1h])) / 0.001 > 14.4

💡 Burn rate = SLO-aware (considers budget, not just threshold)
💡 Multi-window: fast burn (page) + slow burn (ticket)
💡 Eliminates false positives from brief threshold crossings

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Burn rate alerting is an SRE best practice. PCA may test the multi-window concept vs raw threshold alerting.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q204 of 209
How do you configure Alertmanager notification templates to customize Slack messages for anihpj?
A) Templates are written in JSON and stored in Grafana — Alertmanager reads them via API
B) Alertmanager uses fixed templates — you can only customize the webhook URL, not the message content
C) Define custom Go templates in a template file (referenced by `alertmanager.yml` via `templates: ['anihpj.tmpl']`). Templates use `{{ define "slack.anihpj.title" }}...{{ end }}` blocks. Access alert data via `.Alerts`, `.CommonLabels`, `.CommonAnnotations`. For anihpj: custom Slack template with severity-colored attachments, runbook buttons, and dashboard links — much richer than default Slack message
D) Templates use Python Jinja2 syntax — Prometheus uses Python-based templating
✅ Correct Answer: C

Alertmanager templates = Go text/template. Define named templates for title and body per receiver type. Variables: `.Alerts` (all alerts in group), `.Receiver` (receiver name), `.CommonLabels`, `.CommonAnnotations`, `.Status` (firing|resolved). For anihpj: templates add color-coded severity, action buttons, and direct links to dashboards and runbooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Alertmanager Templates — Rich Notifications
# alertmanager.yml
templates:
  - '/etc/alertmanager/templates/anihpj.tmpl'

receivers:
  - name: 'slack-critical'
    slack_configs:
      - title: '{{ template "slack.anihpj.title" . }}'
        text: '{{ template "slack.anihpj.text" . }}'

# anihpj.tmpl (Go template):
{{ define "slack.anihpj.title" }}
  [{{ .Status | toUpper }}] {{ .CommonLabels.severity | toUpper }}
  {{ .CommonLabels.alertname }}
{{ end }}

{{ define "slack.anihpj.text" }}
  *Alert:* {{ .CommonAnnotations.summary }}
  *Description:* {{ .CommonAnnotations.description }}
  *Dashboard:* {{ .CommonAnnotations.dashboard }}
  *Runbook:* {{ .CommonAnnotations.runbook }}
  {{ range .Alerts }}
    • {{ .Labels.instance }}: {{ .Annotations.value }}
  {{ end }}
{{ end }}

COMMON TEMPLATE FUNCTIONS:
  {{ .Alerts | len }}      → number of alerts
  {{ .Alerts.Firing }}     → only firing alerts
  {{ .Alerts.Resolved }}   → only resolved alerts
  {{ range .Alerts }}...{{ end }} → iterate

💡 Go templates, not Jinja2 or JSON
💡 Define blocks: title, text (per receiver type)
💡 Access: .Alerts, .CommonLabels, .Status

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Alertmanager templating with Go templates is a PCA topic. Know template variables and the define/end syntax.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q205 of 209
What is the performance impact difference between recording rules and alerting rules?
A) Both have identical performance impact — they both evaluate PromQL on every evaluation interval
B) Alerting rules are more expensive because they send notifications — recording rules are free
C) Neither has performance impact — Prometheus evaluation is constant-time regardless of rules
D) Recording rules WRITE results as new time series (generates I/O + storage), alerting rules only CHECK conditions (read-only, no storage for the alert itself — alerts go to Alertmanager). Recording rules have STORAGE cost but SAVE CPU (pre-compute once, query many times). Alerting rules have minimal overhead per evaluation. Use recording rules for expensive shared queries; use alerting rules for conditions that fire rarely
✅ Correct Answer: D

Recording rules = write time series (storage cost, CPU savings on repeated queries). Alerting rules = read-only evaluation (minimal cost per check, no storage). Pattern: use recording rules to pre-compute complex expressions used by multiple dashboards AND alerts. The alerting rules then query the pre-computed recording rule output — faster and more consistent.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Recording vs Alerting Rules — Cost Model
RECORDING RULE:
  - record: job:anihpj:error_rate5m
    expr: sum(rate(errors[5m])) / sum(rate(requests[5m]))
    → Writes new series: job:anihpj:error_rate5m
    → Storage cost: 1 new time series × scrape interval
    → CPU savings: computed ONCE, queried MANY times

ALERTING RULE:
  - alert: HighErrorRate
    expr: job:anihpj:error_rate5m > 0.05  ← queries recording rule!
    → No new storage (alert state stored in memory)
    → Minimal CPU (just a comparison)

OPTIMAL PATTERN:
  1. Recording rule: pre-compute complex expression
  2. Dashboard panels: query recording rule output (instant!)
  3. Alerting rules: query recording rule output (fast + consistent)
  → One evaluation, N consumers

WITHOUT RECORDING:
  Dashboard query: sum(rate(...)) / sum(rate(...)) (computed per refresh)
  Alert query:     sum(rate(...)) / sum(rate(...)) (computed per eval)
  → Same expensive query evaluated 2+ times independently

💡 Recording = "compute once, use many times"
💡 Alerting = "check condition, notify if true"
💡 Combine: recording → dashboard + alerting
💡 Recording rules add storage; save CPU

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Recording vs alerting rule cost model is a PCA topic. Know: recording writes data (storage cost), alerting reads only.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q206 of 209
How do you monitor that Alertmanager itself is healthy and processing alerts?
A) Alertmanager sends a daily health report email — no Prometheus monitoring needed
B) Alertmanager exposes its own /metrics endpoint (port 9093). Key metrics: `alertmanager_alerts_total` (alerts processed), `alertmanager_notifications_total` (notifications sent by receiver), `alertmanager_notifications_failed_total` (failed deliveries), `alertmanager_cluster_members` (HA peers). Set up a "meta-alert" in Prometheus: `alertmanager_notifications_failed_total > 0` → "Alertmanager failed to send notification!" — your monitoring should monitor ITSELF
C) Alertmanager health is monitored by external ping only — no internal metrics available
D) Use Grafana's built-in Alertmanager health dashboard — it auto-discovers Alertmanager instances
✅ Correct Answer: B

Monitor your monitoring! Alertmanager exposes /metrics just like any Prometheus target. Meta-alerts: (1) `up{job="alertmanager"} == 0` → Alertmanager is down, (2) `rate(alertmanager_notifications_failed_total[5m]) > 0` → notifications failing, (3) `alertmanager_cluster_members < 3` → HA degraded. Without meta-monitoring, your alerting can fail silently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Meta-Monitoring — Monitor the Monitor
PROMETHEUS SCRAPES ALERTMANAGER /metrics:
  scrape_configs:
    - job_name: 'alertmanager'
      static_configs:
        - targets: ['alertmanager:9093']

KEY ALERTMANAGER METRICS:
  alertmanager_alerts_total{state="firing|resolved"}
  alertmanager_notifications_total{integration="slack|pagerduty|email"}
  alertmanager_notifications_failed_total{integration="..."}
  alertmanager_cluster_members (HA peer count)
  alertmanager_silences_total

META-ALERTS (alert on alerting!):
  - alert: AlertmanagerDown
    expr: up{job="alertmanager"} == 0
    for: 2m
    → "Alertmanager is DOWN — alerts are NOT being delivered!"

  - alert: AlertmanagerNotificationFailure
    expr: rate(alertmanager_notifications_failed_total[5m]) > 0
    for: 5m
    → "Slack/PagerDuty notifications are FAILING!"

  - alert: AlertmanagerHADegraded
    expr: alertmanager_cluster_members < 3
    for: 10m
    → "HA cluster degraded — risk of missed alerts"

💡 Meta-monitoring = monitoring the monitoring system
💡 If Alertmanager dies, WHO alerts you? → Another system or dead man's switch
💡 Alertmanager /metrics → Prometheus → separate dead man's switch alert

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Meta-monitoring is critical. Know Alertmanager's /metrics endpoint and the key health metrics to alert on.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q207 of 209
What is a dead man's switch and why is it essential for Prometheus alerting?
A) A dead man's switch is an alert that fires when Prometheus ITSELF is not running or not evaluating rules — usually an external system (like a second Prometheus, cron job, or cloud monitoring) that checks if Prometheus is alive. If the primary Prometheus dies, the dead man's switch detects the absence and alerts. Without it: Prometheus crashes → no alerts fire → outage goes UNDETECTED. "Who watches the watchmen?" — the dead man's switch does
B) A dead man's switch is a Prometheus alert that fires when a Kubernetes node dies — node-level monitoring
C) A dead man's switch is a manual button on-call presses to acknowledge alerts — it "switches" alert state
D) A dead man's switch is built into Prometheus — no external configuration needed; it auto-detects failure
✅ Correct Answer: A

"Who watches the watchmen?" If your only monitoring is Prometheus and Prometheus dies, you have NO monitoring. A dead man's switch is an EXTERNAL check (separate system) that alerts when Prometheus stops reporting. For anihpj: a cloud function pings `prometheus:9090/api/v1/query?query=up` every minute. If no response for 5 minutes → alert via alternative channel (email directly, not through Alertmanager — it's also dead!).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dead Man's Switch — External Watchdog
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                              │
│  ┌──────────────┐    pings /api/v1/query    ┌────────────┐  │
│  │ Dead Man's   │──────────────────────────→│ Prometheus │  │
│  │ Switch       │←─────── response ────────│  :9090     │  │
│  │ (external)   │                           └────────────┘  │
│  └──────┬───────┘                                           │
│         │                                                   │
│         │ If NO response for 5 minutes:                     │
│         ↓                                                   │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐          │
│  │  SEPARATE notification channel:               │          │
│  │  - Direct email (NOT via Alertmanager!)        │          │
│  │  - Cloud monitoring (AWS CloudWatch/GCP)       │          │
│  │  - Status page update                          │          │
│  │  - Secondary Prometheus in different cluster   │          │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘          │
│                                                              │
│  IMPLEMENTATION OPTIONS:                                     │
│    - AWS Lambda + CloudWatch scheduled event → email/SNS     │
│    - Secondary Prometheus scraping primary Prometheus        │
│    - Cron job: curl + mail on failure                        │
│    - Uptime monitoring service (Pingdom, UptimeRobot)        │
│                                                              │
│  💡 Dead man's switch = EXTERNAL to Prometheus              │
│  💡 "Who watches the watchmen?" — the switch does           │
│  💡 Must use DIFFERENT notification channel                 │
│  💡 If Prometheus dies, Alertmanager dies too — bypass it!  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Dead man's switch is a critical reliability concept. PCA tests understanding of monitoring-the-monitoring.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q208 of 209
How do you set up alerting for anihpj's staging environment differently from production?
A) Use identical alerting rules for staging and production — consistency is more important
B) Disable alerting entirely for staging — it's not production, no alerts needed
C) Use the same rules but route staging alerts to /dev/null — collect metrics, suppress notifications
D) (1) Use the `env` label from external_labels to differentiate. (2) Staging: relaxed thresholds (error rate > 20% instead of 5%), longer `for` (30m instead of 5m), severity always "warning" or "info" (never "critical"). (3) Route staging alerts to a SEPARATE Slack channel (#anihpj-staging-alerts) — never page on-call for staging. (4) Allow staging alerts to be silenced for longer (testing chaos engineering). Production: strict thresholds, short `for`, critical → PagerDuty
✅ Correct Answer: D

Staging alerting should be VISIBLE but NOT interruptive. Different thresholds acknowledge staging is less stable. Different routing ensures staging never pages on-call. For anihpj: staging alerts → #anihpj-staging Slack (developers check during work hours); production alerts → PagerDuty (on-call responds 24/7). The `env` external label enables filtered alerting rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Staging vs Production Alerting Strategy
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PRODUCTION (strict):                                        │
│                                                              │
│  - alert: AnihpjProdHighErrorRate                            │
│    expr: error_rate{env="production"} > 0.05                 │
│    for: 5m                                                   │
│    labels: {severity: "critical", env: "production"}         │
│    → Alertmanager routes to: PagerDuty + Slack #prod-alerts  │
│                                                              │
│  STAGING (relaxed, non-interruptive):                        │
│                                                              │
│  - alert: AnihpjStagingHighErrorRate                         │
│    expr: error_rate{env="staging"} > 0.20                    │
│    for: 30m                                                  │
│    labels: {severity: "warning", env: "staging"}             │
│    → Alertmanager routes to: Slack #staging-alerts ONLY      │
│                                                              │
│  KEY DIFFERENCES:                                            │
│    Threshold:    prod 5%  vs staging 20%                     │
│    for duration: prod 5m  vs staging 30m                     │
│    Severity:     prod critical vs staging warning            │
│    Notification: prod page  vs staging Slack only            │
│    Silences:     prod audit   vs staging can silence 24h+    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 Staging = visible but NOT interruptive
💡 Use external_labels to filter by environment
💡 Different thresholds acknowledge staging instability
💡 NEVER page on-call for staging alerts

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Environment-specific alerting strategy is a practical PCA skill. Know: relaxed thresholds, different routing, no on-call for non-prod.

Alerting & Dashboarding Q209 of 209
Summarize the complete Prometheus alerting pipeline: from metric scrape to on-call notification.
A) Metrics → Grafana → Email — a simple 3-step linear flow
B) (1) Application exposes /metrics (instrumentation + exporters). (2) Prometheus scrapes and stores in TSDB. (3) Alerting rules evaluate PromQL expressions at rule_interval. (4) When expr fires → INACTIVE→PENDING→FIRING state machine with `for` buffer. (5) FIRING alerts sent via POST to Alertmanager /api/v2/alerts. (6) Alertmanager processes: dedup → group → inhibit → silence → route. (7) Routed to receivers: PagerDuty (page), Slack (notify), Email (digest), Webhook (ticket). (8) Dead man's switch monitors the entire pipeline externally. This is the 8-stage pipeline: Instrument → Scrape → Evaluate → Buffer → Send → Process → Route → Monitor
C) Alertmanager scrapes Prometheus for alerts — it's a pull-based pipeline
D) The pipeline is: Application crashes → Prometheus detects down → Alertmanager calls on-call — crash-only monitoring
✅ Correct Answer: B

The 8-stage pipeline: Instrument → Scrape → Evaluate → Buffer → Send → Process → Route → Monitor. Each stage has specific Prometheus/Alertmanager configuration: instrumentation (client libraries + exporters), scrape (prometheus.yml scrape_configs), evaluate (rule_files with alerting rules), buffer (for clause), send (Prometheus → Alertmanager HTTP POST), process (dedup/group/inhibit/silence), route (receivers), monitor (dead man's switch + meta-alerts).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete Alerting Pipeline — 8 Stages
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  STAGE 1: INSTRUMENT                                         │
│  anihpj-api /metrics (django-prometheus + manual counters)   │
│  node_exporter :9100/metrics                                 │
│  postgres_exporter :9187/metrics                             │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 2: SCRAPE                                             │
│  Prometheus scrape_configs → pulls /metrics every 15s        │
│  Stores in TSDB with retention period                        │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 3: EVALUATE                                           │
│  Rule evaluator runs alerting rule PromQL expressions        │
│  rule_files: ['/etc/prometheus/rules/*.yml']                 │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 4: BUFFER (for clause)                                │
│  expr returns data → PENDING state                           │
│  for: 5m elapsed → FIRING state (sent to Alertmanager)       │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 5: SEND                                               │
│  Prometheus POSTs to Alertmanager /api/v2/alerts             │
│  Alertmanager receives JSON alert payload                    │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 6: PROCESS (Alertmanager)                             │
│  Dedup → Group → Inhibit → Silence                           │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 7: ROUTE                                              │
│  Route tree matches labels → receiver                        │
│  critical → PagerDuty | warning → Slack | info → log         │
│                                                              │
│  STAGE 8: MONITOR (external)                                 │
│  Dead man's switch → "Is Prometheus alive?"                  │
│  Meta-alerts → "Is Alertmanager processing?"                 │
│  Scrape health → "Are targets being scraped?"                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 8 stages: Instrument→Scrape→Evaluate→Buffer→Send→Process→Route→Monitor
💡 Each stage has distinct configuration and failure modes
💡 PCA tests the COMPLETE pipeline understanding

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The complete alerting pipeline is the capstone of PCA alerting knowledge. Know all 8 stages end-to-end.
